.....RESPONDENTS
Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to issue a
writ of Mandamus or any other appropriate Writ order or direction, declaring that the action
of the first respondent contained in the impugned circular bearing No. Unit-V(1) 349/2002
dated 08-07-2002, directing the agricultural market committees to levy market fees on the
rice which is milled out by the petitioners from the paddy which has suffered market fee
under the provisions of the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Live stock) Markets Act, 1966, is
arbitrary and illegal and direct the respondents to forbear from levying market fee on the rice
(both rice & boiled) which is produced by the petitioners by milling the paddy which has
suffered payment of market fee when paddy was produced from the agriculturist and further
direct the respondents not to detain the trucks and lorries of the petitioners which are
transporting the rice for want of payment of market fee to respondents, when the petitioners
have already paid the market fee the paddy from which the rice is milled out grant such
other relief as it deems fit in the circumstances of the case.
Counsel for the Petitioners : MR.V.VENKATARAMANA
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 & 2 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE
Counsel for the Respondent No.5 : Mr. POSANI VENKATESWARLU

.....RESPONDENTS
Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
declare the levy and collection of market Fee on Rice as contrary to law declared by
Hon'ble Supreme Court of India by its judgment reported in A.I.R 1983 SC Page
1246 (Sreenivasa General Traders Vs. State of A.P ) and violate of Section 12 of the
A.P (Agricultural Produce and Livestock ) Markets Act, 1966 read with Rule 74 of the
A.P (Agricultural Produce and Live stock ) Markets Act, 1966 and interdict the
respondents by the issue of any writ, direction or order particularly in the nature of
writ of mandamus in the circumstances of the cases declaring circular No.Unit-V(1)349/2002 dated 8.7.2002 issued by respondent No.2 herein as illegal.
Counsel for the Petitioners : MR.D.SUDHAKAR RAO
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 & 2 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE
Counsel for the Respondents No.3 & 4 : MR.BADANA.BHASKARA RAO

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue a writ of mandamus or any other appropriate writ declaring that the action of
the respondents in notifying the Rice as agricultural produce for the purpose of levy
of Market Fee under the Provisions of the A.P. Agricultural (produce & Live Stock )
Market Act, 1966, is arbitrary and illegal, and direct the respondents not to insist the
petitioners to pay the Market Fee on the Rice Milled from out of Paddy, if necessary
by granting a declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within the meaning
of the A.P. Agricultural (Produce & live Stock ) markets Act, 1966.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue a Writ of Mandamus, or any other appropriate writ, order or direction, declaring

the action of the 2
nd
respondent in levying market fee on the sales effected by the
petitioners during the financial year 2002-2003 vide impugned Notice
R.O.C.No.351/2002 dated 23.5.2003 as arbitrary, whimsical, unauthorized, ultra
vires of Section 12 of the Andhra Pradesh (Agricultural Produce and Live Stock)
Market Act, 1966 and contrary to the Rules framed thereunder and set aside the
same as such and pass such other order or orders as may deem fit and proper in the
circumstances of the case.

.....RESPONDENTS
Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue a Writ, order or direction more particularly one in the nature of Writ of
Mandamus declaring that ' Rice ' is not ' notified agricultural produce' within the
meaning of A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Live Stocks) Markets Act, 1966 and the
Collection of market fee from Rice Miller sellers in respect of the rice sold to their
party purchasers without notice, enquiry and on the basis of circular of second

respondent Unit No.V (1) 349/2002 dated 8-7-2002 by misuse of check-posts for the
said purpose is illegal and without jurisdiction.
Counsel for the Petitioner : MR.M.V.DURGA PRASAD
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 & 2 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE
Counsel for the Respondent No.3 : MRS.NANDA R. RAO

3 The Director of Marketing, Government of A.P., BRKR Buildings, Hyderabad
4 The Agricultural Market Committee, Akividu, West Godavari District, rep. by
its Secretary.
.....RESPONDENTS
Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue a Writ of Mandamus, or any other appropriate writ, order or direction, declaring
the action of the respondents in notifying the Rice as Agricultural produce for the
purpose of levy of Market Fee under the provisions of the A.P.Agricultural (Produce
& Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966, is arbitrary and illegal, and direct the respondents
not to insist the petitioners to pay the market fee on the rice milled from out of paddy,
if necessary by granting a declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within
the meaning of the A.P. Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Market Act, 1966.
Counsel for the Petitioner : MR.P.BALAJI VARMA
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 to 3 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE
Counsel for the Respondent No.4 : MR.K.MADHAVA REDDY (SC FOR
AGRL.MRKT.COMTTS).

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to (i)
to declare that Rice is not an agricultural produce and therefore item-2 of schedule-II
of the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Live Stock Market Act, 1966) is bad in law and
ultravires section 2(1) of the Act and (2) to declare that stopping or otherwise
obstructing the movement of rice by the respondents at the checkposts or any either
place, demanding and collecting market fee as from the petitioner is illegal and
beyond the scope of Rule 75 and ultravires Section 12 of the Act by issuing any
appropriate writ, direction or order.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
(1) to declare that Rice is not an "Agricultural Produce" and therefore item 2 of
Schedule II of the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966, is
bad in law and ultravires S.2(1) of the Act and (2) to declare that stopping or
otherwise obstructing the movement of Rice by the Respondents at the Check Posts
or any other places, demanding and collecting Market Fee from the Petitioners, is
illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75 and ultravires Section 12 of the Act, by
issuing any appropriate Writ, Order or Direction appropriate to in the Circumstances
of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
(1) to declare that Rice is not an Agricultural produce and therefore item-2 of
Schedule-II of the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966 and
therefore in accordance with Section 2 (1) of the Act and (2) to declare that stopping
or otherwise obstructing the movement of Rice by the respondents at the
Checkposts or any other place, emanding and collecting Market Fee from the
Petitioners is illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75 and not in accordance with
Section 12 of the Act by issuing any appropriate Writ, direction or order appropriate
in the circumstances.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
issue a writ of mandamus, or any other appropriate writ, order or direction, declaring
that the action of the respondents in notifying the Rice as Agricultural produce for the
purpose of levy of Market Fee under the provisions of the A.P.Agricultural (Produce
& Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966, is arbitrary and illegal and direct the respondents
not to insist the petitioners to pay the market fee on the rice milled from out of paddy,
if necessary by granting a declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within
the meaning of the A.P.Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Market Act, 1966.

circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
to(1) to declare that Rice is not an agricultural produce and therefore item 2 of

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
issue a Writ of Mandamus, or any other appropriate writ, order or direction, declaring
that the action of the respondents in notifying the Rice as Agricultural produce for the
purpose of levy of Market Fee under the provisions of the A.P. Agricultural (Produce
& Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966 , is arbitrary and illegal, and direct the respondents
not to insist the petitioners to pay the market fee on the rice milled from out of paddy,
if necessary by granting a declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within
the meaning of the A.P. Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Market Act, 1966.

circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
issue an appropriate writ, order or direction more particularly one in the nature of
Writ of Mandamus declaring that Item-2 of Schedule-II of the A.P.(Agricultural
Produce and Livestock)Markets Act, 1966 is not in accordance with Section-2(1)of
the said Act is illegal, arbitrary and unjust and further declare that rice is not an
agricultural produce and consequently direct the respondents not to stop or
otherwise obstruct the movement of rice at the check posts or any other place for
collection of market fee from the petitioners.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue an appropriate writ, order or direction more particularly one in the nature of
Writ of Mandamus declaring the action of the respondents in notifying Rice as
Agricultural Produce for the purpose of levy of Market fee under the provisions of the
Andhra Pradesh (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966 is arbitrary,
and illegal and direct the Respondents not to collect any market fee on the Rice from
the Petitioners/Sellers either by demand or by way of obstructing the lorries at Check
Posts and other vehicles carrying Rice in the District, if necessary, declaring that the
Rice is not an agricultural produce within the meaning of the Andhra Pradesh
(Agricultural Produce and Live Stock) Markets Act, 1976 and pass such other or
further order or orders in the circumstances of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the

circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
declare the Rice is not an agricultural produce and therefore item 2 of Schedule - II of the
A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1958 and therefore not in
accordance with sec 2(1) of the Act and strike down item 2 of Schedule II of the Act and (2)
to declare that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement of Rice by the respondents
at the check posts or any other place, demanding and collecting Market Fee from the
petitioners is illegal, and beyond the scope of Rule 75 and not in accordance with action 12
of the Act by issuing Writ of Mandamus or any other Writ or direction or order appropriate in
the circumstances of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased (!) to
declare that rice is not an agricultural produce and therefore item 2 of Schedule-11
of the A.P.(Agricultural Produce and Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966 and therefore not
in accordance with Section 2(1) of the Act and strike down item 2 of Schedule-II of
the Act and (2) to declare that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement of
rice by the respondents at the check posts or any other place, demanding and
collecting Market Fee from the petitioners is illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75
and not in accordance with Section 12 of the Act by issuing Writ of Mandamus or
any other writ, direction or appropriate order in the circumstances and pass such

other order or orders as deemed fit in the circumstances of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
declare that rice is not an agricultural produce and therefrom item 2 of Schedule -II of
the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966 and therefore not in
accordance with Section 2(`1) of the Act and strike down item 2 of Schedule - II of
the Act and (2) to declare that stopping or otherwise obsructing the movement of rice
by the respondents at the check aposts or any other place, demanding and
collecting Market Fee from the Petitioners is illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75
and not in accordance with Sec. 12 of the Act by issuing Writ of Mandamus or any
other Writ, direction or appropriate order in the circumstances of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
To issue a writ of mandamus or any other appropriate writ declaring that the action of
the respondents in notifying the Rice as Agricultural produce for the purpose of levy
of Market Fee under the Provisions of the A.P.Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock)
Markets Act, 1966, is arbitrary and illegal, and direct the respondents not to insist the
petitioners to pay the market fee on the rice milled from out of paddy, if necessary by
granting a declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within the meaning of
the A.P.Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Market Act, 1966.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
declare that Rice is not an agricultural produce and therefore item 2 of Schedule-II of
the A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966 and therefore not in
accordance with Section 2 (1)of the Actand strike down item 2 of Schedule-II of the
Act and (2) to declare that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement of Rice
by therespondents at the check posts or any other place, demanding and collecting
Market Fee from the Petitioners is illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75 and not
in accordance with Section 12 of the Act by issuing writ of mandamus or any other
writ, direction or appropriate order.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to to
issue a Writ order or direction especially one in the nature of Writ of Mandamus

declaring the action of the respondents in notifying the Rice as agricultural produce
for the purpose of levy of Market Fee under the provisions of the A.P.(Agricultural
Produce and Live Stock) Markets Act 1966 is arbitrary and illegal and direct the
respondents not to insist the petitioners to pay the Market Fee on the Rice milled
from out Paddy if necessary by granting a declaration that the rice is not an
agricultural produce within the meaning of the A.P.(Agricultural Produce an Live
Stock) Markets Act 1966
Counsel for the Petitioners:MR.CH.RAVINDER
Counsel for the Respondents : MR.BADANA.BHASKARA RAO FOR R3
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 & 2 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE

that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement of Rice, Paddy and Pulses and other

and demanding and collecting Market Fee from the petitioners before determination of

and beyond the scope of Rule 75 and not in accordance with Section 12 of the A.P.[Agrl.
Produce and Livestock] Market Act,1966 by issuing writ of mandamus or any other writ,
direction or appropriate order in the circumstances and pass such other order or orders as
are deemed fit and proper in the circumstances of the case.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue an appropriate Writ, order or direction more particularly one in the lnature of
Writ of Mandamus declaring the action of the respondents in notifying rice as
agricultural produce for the purpose of levy of Market fee under the provisions of the
Andhra Pradesh (Agricultural Produce and Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966 is arbitrary
and illegal and direct the respondents not to collect any market fee on the Rice from
the petitioner/sellers either by demand or by way of obstructing the lorries at Check
Posts and other vehicles carrying Rice in the District, if necessary declaring that the
Rice is not an agricultural produce within the meaning fo the Andhra Pradesh
(Agricultural Produce and Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966.

Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue an appropriate order or direction more particularly one in nature of Writ of
Mandamus declaring that the action of the respondents in notifying the Rice as
Agricultural Produce for the purpose of levy of Market Fee under the provisions of
the A.P.Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Markets Act, 1966, as illegal, arbitrary
and consequently direct the respondents not to insist the petitioners to pay the
market fee on the rice milled from out of paddy, if necessary by granting a
declaration that Rice is not an agricultural produce within the meaning of the
A.P.Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) Market Act 1966.

circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
declare that Rice is not an Argicultural Produce and therefore item 2 of Schedule-II
of the AP. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966 and therefore not
in accordance with section 2(1) of the Act and strike down item 2 of Schedule-II of
the Act and (2) to declare that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement of
rice by the Respondents at the Check posts or any other place, demanding and
collecting market Fee from the Petitioner is illegal and beyond the scope of Rule 75
and not in accordance with section 12 of the Act by issuing Writ of Mandamus or any
other Writ, direction or appropriate order in the circumstances of the cases.

.....RESPONDENT(S)
Petition under Article 226 of the constitution of India praying that in the
circumstances stated in the Affidavit filed herein the High Court may be pleased to
issue an appropriate order or direction more particularly one in nature of Writ of
Mandamus declaring that the action of therespondent in notifiying the Rice as
Agricultural produce for the purpose of levy of Market fee under the provision of the
A.P. Agricultural (Produce & Live Stock) markets Act, 1966 as illegal, arbitrary and
consequently direct the respondents not to insist the petitioners to pay the market fee
on the rice milled from out to paddy if necessary by granting a declaration that Rice
is not an agricultural produce within the meaning of the A.P. Agricultural (Produce &
Live Stock)Market Act 1966.
Counsel for the Petitioner : MR.KARNAMRAMESH
Counsel for the Respondents No.1 & 2 : GP FOR AGRICULTURE
Counsel for the Respondents No. 3 & 4 : Mr.BADANA BHASKAR RAO

COMMON ORDER : (per Hon’ble Sri Bilal Nazki, J)
There are two sets of Writ Petitions in this batch of Writ Petitions. Both
sets have been heard together. There are some common questions
of law and are being disposed of by this common order. For the one
set, Writ Petition No.3327 of 2004 shall be taken as a representative
writ petition and for other set, Writ Petition No.22867 of 2003 shall be
taken as a representative writ petition. Both the sets of writ petitions
revolve around the interpretations to be placed on various provisions
of A.P. (Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Markets Act, 1966
(hereinafter referred to as ‘the Act’).
In Writ Petition No.3327 of 2004, the following prayer has been
made;
“To declare that stopping or otherwise obstructing the movement
of Rice, Paddy & Pulses and other Agricultural Produce by the
Respondents at any check posts or any other place in the State
and demanding and collecting Market Fee from the petitioners
before determination of liability is made under Section 12-A, 12-B

and 12-C of the Act is illegal, and without jurisdiction and beyond
the scope of Rule 75 and not in accordance with Section 12 of the
A.P.(Agricultural Produce and Livestock) Market Act, 1966 by
issuing Writ of Mandamus or any other writ, direction or
appropriate order in the circumstances and pass such other order
or orders as are deemed fit and proper in the circumstances of the
case.”
The petitioners in this case contended that they are licensees
under Section 7 of the Act. They submitted that they are dealers in
rice, paddy, pulses and some other agricultural products. The
petitioners further contended that they buy and sell rice, paddy,
pulses and in terms of Section 12 of the Act, they are liable to pay
Market fee when the purchaser is not identified. During the course of
trade, the petitioners purchase or sell various agricultural products
and during these transactions, they have necessarily to transport
these products from one place to the other within State as well as
outside the State. The petitioners questioned the action of
respondents in stopping the vehicles during the transport at various
check points set up by them. They also questioned the respondents’
power to demand and collect market fee on the agricultural products.
The collection of fee at check posts without any determination with
regard to the liability and before time expires for filing the return for
assessment, was illegal. It is the case of petitioners that the
respondents are doing an illegal act by obstructing the free
movement of agricultural commodity by stopping the vehicles at
check posts established under Rule 75 of the Rules framed under the
Act. It is further submitted that the 1
st
respondent amended Rule 74
of the Rules by G.O.Ms.No.459 Agriculture and Cooperation (Mktg.II)
Dept., dated 23.12.2002, by which it was declared that fee if paid to a
market committee, shall not be collected by any other market
committee in the State, subject to production of proof of payment of
market fee. It is contended that by this amendment, the effect of the
judgment of the Hon’ble Supreme Court reported in Commissioner of
Income-Tax, West Bengal, Calcutta V. Benoy Kumar Sahas Roy,
was sought to be nullified.

Counter has been filed by the 3
rd
respondent-Secretary of
Agricultural Market Committee. It is submitted that the Act was
enacted to regulate the purchase and sale of agricultural produce,
livestock and produce of livestock for the benefit and to protect
interest of the farmers/producers and to create an atmosphere of
having competitive price for the produce by eliminating the middle
men and unholy trade practices. The State Government exercising
powers under section 33 of the Act, framed Rules, 1969 and in terms
of Section 3 of the Act, the Government issued notification vide
G.O.Ms.No.2095 dated 29.10.1968 and published it in Gazette on
07.11.1968, declaring the notified area and notified agricultural
produce, livestock and produce of livestock as specified in Schedule
II appended to the Act. Thereafter, the Government issued
notifications declaring the notified market area and the notified
agricultural produce in respect of different market committees. As per
said notifications, rice is one of the notified agricultural produces. It is
further submitted that in terms of Section 12 of the Act, the Market
Committee has power to levy market fee on the notified agricultural
produce purchased or sold in the notified market area on such rate
as specified in the bye-laws. By-law 24 of the bye-laws lays down
that the Market Committee was entitled to collect market fee at the
rate of 1 per cent on advalorem basis. Sub-Section 2 of Section 12 of
the Act contemplates that the market fee shall be paid by the
purchaser of the produce and proviso to Sub-Section 2 of Section 12
of the Act lays down that where the purchaser was not identifiable,
the fee shall be payable by the seller and as per Section 12(E) of the
Act, any trader objecting the order of assessment passed by the
assessing authority, can file an appeal before the Regional Joint
Director of Marketing. It is further contended that explanation 1 of
Section 12 of the Act contemplates that all the notified agricultural
produce taken out of a notified market area, shall unless the contrary
is proved, be presumed to have been purchased or sold within that
area. In terms of Rule 75, no person shall transport any notified
agricultural produce purchased or sold in the notified market area
from the limits thereof except on production of receipt for payment of

prescribed fee with the market committee concerned and the
authorized employees of the market committee has the power to stop
and check the vehicles.
The case of the petitioners in nutshell is that unless there is an
assessment, there cannot be any demand and the learned Counsel
appearing for respondents submit that this question has already been
decided by this Court in a judgment reported in M/s.Godavari Trading
Company, Palakol and another Vs. Agricultural Market Committee,
Palakol and others. This is a judgment of the Division Bench of this
Court. Before going to this judgment, it will be necessary to go
through the scheme of the Act as far as this argument is concerned.
Section 2 of the Act pertains to the definitions. Section 3 pertains to
the declaration of notified areas. Under Section 4, the market
committees are constituted and declaration of notified market area is
made. Under Section 5, the composition of market committees is laid
down. Under Section 7, trading etc., of notified agricultural produce,
livestock and products of livestock in the notified area is prescribed
except under and in accordance with the conditions of a license
granted by the market committee. Section 12 gives the power under
which the fee can be levied by the market committee. It reads as
under;
“12. Levy of fees by the market committee :- (1) The
market committee shall levy fees on any notified
agricultural produce, livestock or products of livestock
purchased or sold in the notified market area *[at such
rate, not exceeding *[two rupees] as may be specified in
the bye-laws] for every hundred rupees of the aggregate
amount for which the notified agricultural produce,
livestock or products of livestock is purchased or sold,
whether for cash or deferred payment or other valuable
consideration.
Explanation I :- For the purposes of this section, all
notified agricultural produce, livestock or products of
livestock taken out of a notified market area shall, unless
the contrary is proved, be presumed to have been

purchased or sold within such area.
Explanation II :- In the determination of the amount of
fees payable under this Act, fractions of ten paise equal to
or exceeding five paise shall be counted as ten paise and
other fractions of ten paise shall be disregarded.
(2) The fees referred to in sub-section (1) shall be paid by
the purchaser of the notified agricultural produce, livestock
or products of livestock:
Provided that where the purchaser cannot be identified
the fees shall be paid by the seller.”
Section 12-A reads as under;
“Section 12-A: Submission of return of turnover by
trader:- Every trader in the notified area, who is liable to
pay fees under Section 12, shall submit such return or
returns relating to his turnover in such manner, within such
period and to such authority, as may be specified by the
market committee in its bye-laws.
Explanation:- For the purposes of Sections 12-A to 12-G
(both inclusive) the terms-i. “market fees” shall mean the fees levied under sub-section (1) of Section 12;
ii. “turnover” shall mean the aggregate amount for which
the notified agricultural produce, livestock or
products of livestock, are purchased or sold,
whether for cash or deferred payment or
other valuable consideration.”
Section 12-B reads;
“Section 12-B: Assessment of market fees :- (1) If the
assessing authority is satisfied that any return submitted under
Section 12-A is correct and complete, it shall assess the amount
of market fees payable by the trader on the basis thereof; but if the
return appears to it to be incorrect or incomplete, it shall, after
giving the trader an opportunity of providing the correctness and
completeness of the returns submitted by him and after making
such inquiry as it considers necessary, assess to the best of its
judgment the amount of market fees due from the trader. An

assessment under this section shall however, be made only within
a period of three years from the expiry of the year to which the
assessment relates.
(2) Where the return submitted by a trader includes the turnover or
any of the particulars thereof which would not have been disclosed
but for an inspection of accounts, registers or other documents of
the trader made by an officer authorized under this Act before the
submission of such returns, the assessing authority may, after
giving an opportunity to the trader for making a representation in
this behalf, treat such return to be an incorrect or incomplete
return within the meaning of sub-section (1) and proceed to take
action on that basis.
(3) While making an assessment to the best of Judgment under
sub-section (1) the assessing authority may also direct the trader
to pay, in addition to the market fees assessed a penalty equal to
two times the market fees due on the turnover that was not
disclosed by the trader in his return.
(4) Where any trader liable to pay market fees under this Act-i. fails to submit return before the date specified in that behalf;
or
ii. produce the accounts, registers and other documents after
inspection; or
iii. submits a return subsequent to the date of inspection;
the assessing authority may, at any time within a period of three
years from the expiry of the year to which the assessment relates,
after issuing a notice to the trader, and after making such inquiry
as it considers necessary, assess to the best of its judgment, the
amount, of market fees due from the trader, on his turnover for
that year and may direct him to pay in addition to the market fees
so assessed, a penalty equal to two times the market fees due.
(5) Where, for any reason, the whole or any part of the turnover of
the trader has escaped assessment to market fees or has been
under-assessed or assessed at a rate lower than the correct rate,
the assessing authority may, at any time within a period of three
years from the date on which any order of assessment was served
on the trader,-
a. determine to the best of its judgment the turnover that has
escaped assessment and assess the turnover so
determined;

b. assess the correct amount of market fees payable on the
turnover that has been under-assessed;
c. assess at the correct rate the turnover that has been
assessed at a lower rate, after issuing a notice to the
trader and after making such inquiry as it considers
necessary. The assessing authority, in addition to the
market fee as assessed, also direct the trader to pay a
penalty equal to two times the market fees.”
In the judgment referred to above (2 supra), the contention was that
under Section 12(2) of the Act, the market fee was payable by the
purchaser and therefore it could not be demanded from the traders
who have sold the rice. This argument was replied by the Court in
paras 10 and 10(A) as under;
“10. It is next contended that under Section 12(2) of the Act the
market fee is payable by the purchaser of the notified
agricultural produce and, therefore, it cannot be demanded from
the traders who have sold the rice. Sub-Section (2) of Section
12 of the Act reads as follows:-
“12. (1) … … …
(2) The fees referred to in Sub-section 1 shall be paid by the
purchaser of the notified agricultural produce, livestock or
products of livestock.
Provided where the purchaser cannot be identified, the fee
shall be paid by the seller.”
Having regard to the said sub-section, there can be no doubt that
the market fee leviable under Section 12(1) is primarily to be paid
by the purchaser of the notified agricultural produce. Only where
the purchaser cannot be identified, “the fee shall be paid by the
seller”. Agricultural produce may be sold in regulated markets only
by a licenced trader. In all regulated markets when agricultural
produce is sold bills have to be issued wherein the name of the
seller and purchaser are to be recorded. Condition No.8 of the
licence lays down that the trader shall collect market fees from the
purchaser and remit the same to the market committee. Where a
licensee sells notified Agricultural produce, Livestock or Products
of Livestock of his own he shall collect market fees from the
purchaser and remit the same to the Market Committee. Condition
No.5 of the licence further enjoins that licensee shall pay and also

facilitate the collection of fees under Section 12 of the Act due to
the market Committee as and when it becomes due as per the
provisions of the Act, Rules and bye-laws of the Market
Committee. Where the licensee functions as Commission Agent,
Condition No.6 of his licence enjoins that he shall collect the
market fee from the Purchaser and remit the same to the market
committee and shall deliver goods to the purchaser after satisfying
himself fully that the purchaser has paid the market fee. Condition
No.3 of the licence further stipulates that the licensee shall submit
to the Committee not later than 25
th of every month true and
correct information regarding the purchases, sale and storage of
Agricultural produce by him in the preceding month to enable the
market committee to determine the fee payable by him and collect
the said fee under Section 12 of the said Act, due to it on such
transactions as per the provisions of the Act, Rules and bye-laws.
The form of the licence prescribed under Rule 48 is intended to
carry out the purposes of the Act. Though the liability to pay the
market fee is on the purchaser of the goods, the licensee is so to
say, constituted as an agent for the collection of the market fee
from the purchaser. Though the primary liability to pay market fee
is on the purchaser, if the market fee is collected by the licensee,
he will become liable to pay the same to the Committee. When the
petitioners are enjoined under the licence to collect fees and remit
the same, they cannot be absolved of their liability on the ground
that under Section 12(2) of the Act only the purchaser of the
notified agricultural produce is liable to pay the tax and that only in
the event of the purchaser not being traceable and not until then. It
is not as if the Act wholly exempts the sellers from payment of
market fee. Even the sellers are, under the proviso, liable to pay
the market fee. Of course that is dependent upon the purchaser
not being identifiable. When in order to carry out the purposes of
the Act, under the Rules the licensee is required to collect the
market fees and remit the same to the market committee, he
cannot be permitted to turn round and plead that he is not liable to
pay the tax. Of course, if the seller was not required to collect the
market fee and has not in fact collected the same, he would not be
liable to pay. But that is because the seller was not enjoined to
collect market fee and not because the primary liability is on the
seller and not on purchaser. This aspect however would be
relevant with reference to the period during which the Government
had decided not to levy market fee on the purchase and sale of
rice derived from the paddy which had already suffered market fee
and issued instructions accordingly to the Director of Marketing
who in his turn advised all the market committees in the State not
to collect market fee thereon from 1-4-1973. Although the licence
enjoined collection of market fee by the licenced traders from the

purchasers, during the relevant period, they were under the
instructions of the Government and advise of the Director of
Marketing not to collect market fee on the rice derived from paddy
which had already suffered market fee and in fact they were
obeying such instructions. If under these instructions, as
discussed elsewhere, the licenced traders had not collected
market fee, they cannot be said to have contravened any
conditions of the licence, nor can they be held liable to pay the
market fee under Section 12(2) of the Act. Where the liability is
imposed by the provisions of the Act and conditions of the licence,
in the absence of any instructions to the contrary, the licenced
trader cannot escape the liability to pay market fee on the ground
that sub-section 2 of Section 12 of the Act imposes the liability to
pay market fee on the purchasers. But it is clear until it is
established that the purchaser is not identifiable, market fee
cannot be recovered from the sellers.
10A. Sec.12 is the charging Section which authorizes the levy of
market fee on agricultural produce. Rule 74 of the rules makes
provision in this behalf and regulates procedure for collection of
market fee leviable. Bye-law 24 of the Market Committee
stipulates when and at what points and at what rates market fee
may be levied and collected.”
The contention that the fee has to be paid only by the
purchaser and not the seller and can only be collected after the
assessment is made, has not been accepted by this Court earlier and
cannot be accepted in view of Rule 74. Rule 74 lays down;
“74. Market Fees :- [(1) The fees leviable under sub-section (1) of
Section 12 on notified agricultural produce, livestock and products
of livestock if paid to a market committee within the State shall not
be collected by another Market Committee when such notified
agricultural produce, livestock or products of livestock are brought
into the notified market area of another market committee for the
purpose of processing, pressing, packing, storage, export and on
sales effected in the course of commercial transactions between
the licensed traders, and the licensed traders and consumers
subject to production of such evidence as may be prescribed in
the bye-laws about the payment or market fees from where it was
brought.]

Provided that the fees shall be levied on the notified agricultural
produce, livestock or products of livestock are sold in auction or in
other manner prescribed in the bye-laws in the market either
directly or through commission agents even though purchased
already in the same market or some other market or place within
the State.
(2) Such fees shall be leviable as soon as the notified agricultural
produce, livestock and products of livestock is purchased or sold
by a licensee. The notified agricultural produce, livestock or
products of livestock shall be deemed to have been purchased or
sold after the notified commodity has been weighed or measured
or counted or when it is taken out of the notified market area.”
This question has already been decided by this Court by an
elaborate judgment and we have not been given any reasons which
would make us disagree with the law laid down by the Division Bench
of this Court.
The respondents have also relied on a judgment reported in
Raghunath Cotton and Oil Product Ltd. V. Director of Marketing, A.P.,
Hyderabad & another. It is also a Division Bench judgment of this
Court. Again the argument was same which is made before us. After
extracting section 12 of the Act and Rule 74(2), the Court held in para
7;
“7. A perusal of sub-rule (2) of Rule 74 extracted above makes it
clear that it incorporates the presumption contained in Explanation
I to Section 12 of the Act. It must be noted that neither Explanation
I to Rule 12 nor sub-rule (2) of Rule 74 extends the presumption to
the goods which are being transported through the Market Area.
Inasmuch as the liability to pay market fee arises on sale or
purchase of any notified agricultural produce, livestock or products
of livestock, the said Explanation and the said sub-rule raise a
presumption which is rebuttable that when any notified agricultural
produce, livestock or products of livestock are taken out of the
market area then such commodities will be presumed to have
been purchased or sold within such area. This presumption does
not arise in a case where, admittedly the commodities referred to
above are purchased in a different State and are being
transported.”

It also laid emphasis on Rule 75 and recorded its findings in
para 9 as under;
“9. Sub-rule (1) of Rule 75 injuncts that any person shall transport
any notified agricultural products, livestock or products of
livestock purchased or sold in the notified market area from the
limits thereof, only on production of the receipt for the payment of
prescribed fees in respect of such notified agricultural produce,
livestock or products of livestock to the market committee
concerned. It is clear that this sub-rule is framed to check evasion
of market fee and to safe-guard the interest of the revenue of the
Market Committee and enjoins that the transportation of the above
said commodities from the limits of the Market Committee should
be only on production of receipt of payment of the prescribed fee.
Now sub-rule (2) of Rule 75 authorises the employees of Market
Committee to stop and check all vehicles suspected to carry
unauthorisedly any notified agricultural produce, livestock or
products of livestock from any place within the limits of any
notified market area. This sub-rule cannot be diversed from sub-rule (1) which has to be read in conjunction with sub-rule (2) of
Rule 75. Thus read it becomes clear that this Rule is also framed
to check evasion of market fee by empowering the authorized
employees to check the vehicles which are suspected to transport
any notified agricultural produce, livestock or products of livestock
from any place within the limits of any notified market area. Unless
the suspicion of the authorized officer of the Market Committee
relates to non-payment of market fee in respect of sale or
purchase of the commodities referred to above, in the notified
area, sub-rule (2) of Rule 75 cannot be called in aid. Rule 75 also
does not deal with the transportation of the goods purchased from
outside the State and passing through any market area. Of course,
this does not mean that the employees of the second respondent
are not entitled to satisfy themselves that the goods are purchased
from any place outside the State or in other words that the goods
were not purchased or sold within the concerned notified market
area.”
However, the petitioners have relied on another Division Bench
judgment of this Court reported in Modern Nutrition Company,
Hyderabad V. Agricultural Market Committee and others. In para 14
of the judgment it is laid down;
“14. For the reasons aforementioned, the appeal is allowed. The
impugned judgment is set aside, consequentially the writ petition is

allowed and it is declared that respondents are not entitled to
realize any market fee from any person at the check-post, who is
not shown to have purchased or sold any agricultural produce,
etc., upon which fee is leviable under Section 12 of the Act. Any
fee already realized in the absence of the above being established
in the case of the writ petitioner-appellant shall be refunded to it.”
We do not find that this judgment is in conflict with the earlier
judgments to which a reference has been made hereinabove.
For these reasons, we do not feel that these writ petitions can
be allowed. They deserves to be dismissed and are accordingly
dismissed.
Now, coming to the second set of Writ Petitions, of which, we
have taken Writ Petition No.22867 of 2003 as a representative writ
petition, though the respondents contend that the controversy raised
in these writ petitions is also covered in the earlier batch of writ
petitions, but Mr.C.V.Mohan Reddy appearing for some of the
petitioners, would contend that rice is not an agricultural produce at
all and therefore the market fee could not be levied on it. According to
him, this question was not considered in earlier judgments. These
Writ Petitions have been filed seeking a mandamus declaring the
action of respondents in notifying the rice as agricultural produce for
the purpose of the Act, as arbitrary and illegal. The learned Counsel
would argue that in terms of Section 2, agricultural produce has been
defined as,
“Agricultural produce means anything produced from land in the
course of agriculture or horticulture and includes forest produce or
any produce of like nature either processed or unprocessed and
declared by the Government by notification, to be agricultural
produce for the purposes of this Act.”
There is no dispute that rice has been declared by the Government
by notification, to be agricultural produce for the purpose of this Act.
But what the learned counsel for petitioners would submit is that by a
bare reading of the definition of ‘agricultural produce’, it would

become clear that agricultural produce would mean anything
produced from the land in the course of agriculture or horticulture. It
would include forest produce or any produce of like nature either it
was processed or unprocessed. According to the learned Counsel, a
product can be agricultural produce within the definition either when it
is processed or when it is unprocessed. Both the processed and
unprocessed product, which is produced from the land, cannot be
subjected to levy of market fee under the Act. According to him, rice
is processed product derived from paddy and paddy is derived from
land in the course of agriculture. The learned Counsel would
contend, if paddy is notified to be agricultural produce, that would
mean a produce which was produced in the course of agriculture,
was notified in an unprocessed stage. After paddy was declared to
be an agricultural produce, rice could not be declared agricultural
produce. The contra would also be correct that if paddy had not been
declared to be an agricultural produce, rice could have been declared
as agricultural produce. He lays emphasis on the words in the
definition, “produce of like nature either processed or unprocessed”.
The learned Counsel Mr.Mohan Reddy contends, the purpose of
using those words is to set the limitations of meaning without altering
the normal meaning. For the fact that rice was not produced from the
land, it would not fall within the meaning of ‘agricultural produce’. It
would however fall in the meaning of ‘agricultural produce’ if it were
processed. He further submits that the word “or” in the phrase “either
processed or unprocessed” in Section 2 of the Act cannot be read as
“and”. Mr.Mohan Reddy contends that if the intention of the
Legislature was to make rice and paddy both subject to the Act, then
the words would have been, “produce of like nature processed and
unprocessed”. According to him, “either or” creates an exclusive
division between two alternatives and demands a choice. According
to him, whenever the phrase “either or” is used, that would mean
either of the two and not both. Therefore according to him, on bare
reading of the phrase “either processed or unprocessed”, the
Government has no power to notify both the products i.e. rice and
paddy as agricultural produce within the meaning of the Act. He has

referred to dictionary meaning of the word “either”. Blacks Law
Dictionary 6
th Edition, Page 516 defines “either” as,
“Either : Each of two; the one and the other; one or the other of
two alternatives; one of two. Often used, however, with reference
to more than two, in which case it may mean “each” or “any”; but
does not mean “all”.”
He has also referred to Strouds dictionary, in which the meaning of
the word “either” is given as under;
“Either : (1) “Originally, ‘either’ had much of the meaning of
‘both’. For some centuries, however, its normal meaning has been,
‘one or other’, see Murray’s English Dictionary. Certainly that is its
prima facie meaning at the present time” (per Rigby L.J., Re
Pickworth, 68 L.J.Ch.328; but see per Williams L.J., ibid.).
(2) Where there is a devise to two, “but in case either one of them
should die without children that share to go to the other,” and both
die without children, the property on the death of the one who died
first goes to the other (Drennan v. Andrew, 36 L.J.Ch.1). But
where there was a gift for life to A, with a vested interest after her
death to B and C, “and if either of them shall be then dead, upon
trust for the survivor of them absolutely,” and both died in the
lifetime of A; held (Rigby L.J., diss.) that the vested interest of B
and C was not divested, and that their representatives took equally
(Re Pickworth [1899] 1 ch. 642; in which case were considered
Browne v. Kenyon, 3 Mad. 410; White v. Baker, 29 L.J. Ch.577;
Harison v. Foreman, 5 Ves. 207; and Scurfield v. Howes, 3
Bro.C.C.90). Observe, that the decision in Re Pickworth refused to
read “either” as “both”, or “survivor” as “longest liver.”
(3) In Re Hill to Chapman the question turned on the following
phrase in a will, “in case of the death of either of them”; on which
Brett M.R., observed, “I think the word ‘either’ means ‘one’, and
not ‘the other’” (54 L.J. Ch.597). So, in Sharp v. Sharp (2B. & Ald.
405; stated, Lewin, 776), a power to appoint new trustees “in case
either” of the appointed trustees should die, etc., “either” was held
to mean “some one” of the trustees, not “all” of them. “Should
either of my said daughters die,” “plainly means ‘any’” (per Balfour
L.P., Anderson’s Trustees v. Miller, 37 S.L.R.231).”

He has also referred to the judgment reported in Messrs. Govindram
Ramprasad V. Assessing Authority (Sales Tax) and another. In this
case, the Judges were confronted with interpretation of a provision.
The relevant portion of the provision is quoted in the judgment as;
“Turn over means the aggregate amount for which goods are either
sold or supplied for the payment received in respect of a contract
by a dealer…………..”.
Then the Court stated;
“Now the word ‘either’ may be a pronoun or a conjunction or an
adverb. When it is used with the word ‘or’ it is used as a
conjunction. In Currie v. Glen, 54 Com-W LR 445 (B), Starks J.
observed at page 453 that the word ‘either’ is not a technical word.
It is a distributive word and may mean one of two or each of two
depending upon the subject matter and the context.
The learned Judge gave numerous examples of how ‘either’ is
used. In the Webster’s dictionary it is said that when the words
‘either’ and ‘or’ are used they indicate a choice between one thing
or another. According to the Webster’s dictionary they are then
being used as a conjunction, and examples are given like ‘Either
he does or I do”, “either in late spring or early summer”. From
these examples it is deduced in the dictionary that there is choice
between the one or the other of the things mentioned. They are not
conjunctively placed so as to be read together. They are to be
read as alternatives from which the choice or a selection must be
made.”
Then the Court went on to say in para 9,
“Reading the Section in the light of these observations, it is quite
clear that the words “either”, “or” are not used in any technical
sense. They are used to denote two states of affairs in which a
turnover would be found. Referring to the definition, therefore,
‘turnover’ means the aggregate amount for which goods are either
sold or supplied. It would be clear from this that there is either to
be a sale of the goods or the goods have to be supplied. The short
question is whether the phrase “for the payment received in
respect of a contract” can legitimately be taken to qualify the word
“sold”.”

Counsel for petitioners Mr.C.V.Mohan Reddy has also referred to a
judgment reported in M/s.Ranchhoddas Atmaram and another V. The
Union of India and others. This is a judgment by a Constitutional
Bench of the Supreme Court. The Constitutional Bench was
interpreting following words in the Statute,
“Any person concerned in any such offence shall be liable to a
penalty not exceeding three times the value of the goods or not
exceeding one thousand rupees.”
The Supreme Court framed the following question;
“The question is whether, in imposing a penalty, the conditions laid
down in both the alternative clauses joined by the word “or” have
to be fulfilled or the condition in any one of them only ?”
In Paras 13 and 14, it laid down;
“(13). It is clear that if the words form an affirmative sentence,
then the condition of one of the clauses only need be fulfilled. In
such a case “or” really means “either” “or”. In the Shorter Oxford
Dictionary one of the meanings of the word “or” is given as “A
particle co-ordinating two (or more) words, phrases or clauses
between which there is an alternative.” It is also there stated, “The
alternative expressed by “or” is emphasized by prefixing the first
member or adding after the last, the associated adv. EITHER.” So,
even without “either”, “or” alone creates an alternative. If,
therefore, the sentence before us is an affirmative one, then we
get two alternatives, any one of which may be chosen without the
other being considered at all. In such a case it must be held that a
penalty exceeding Rs.1,000 can be imposed.
(14). If, however, the sentence is a negative one, then the position
becomes different. The word “or” between the two clauses would
then spread the negative influence over the clause following it.
This rule of grammar is not in dispute. In such a case the
conditions of both the clauses must be fulfilled and the result
would be that the penalty that can be imposed can never exceed
Rs.1,000.”
Now, in the light of these judgments, the learned Counsel

Mr.C.V.Mohan Reddy wants that either the rice or the paddy can be
made subject matter of market fee under the Act. When a phrase
“either or” is used, that gives a choice and that would mean any one
of the two and not both. We do not agree with the construction that is
being tried to be placed on Section 2(i) of the Act, which in our view,
is in three parts. The first part would lay down that ‘agricultural
produce’ means anything produced from the land in the course of
agriculture or horticulture. This part of the definition of ‘agricultural
produce’ concludes at that. And then comes the second part, which
lays down that it includes forest produce or any produce of like nature
either processed or unprocessed. Third part of the Section would
mean, declared by the Government by notification, to be agricultural
produce for the purposes of this Act. For the purposes of
understanding of this Section, in our view, agricultural produce would
mean;
1. Anything produced from the land in the course of
agriculture or horticulture.
2. Forest produce or any produce of like nature either
processed or unprocessed.
3. Any processed or unprocessed produce declared by the
Government by notification, to be an agricultural
produce for the purpose of this Act.
The declaration by notification, by the Government, applies to
all the produces. It is the choice for the Government to declare any
produce which may be processed or may be unprocessed.
Therefore, both processed and unprocessed agricultural produce
can be agricultural produce provided, it is notified in terms of the Act
for the purposes of the Act, by the Government.
In order to further cement our opinion, we would like to refer to
the statement of objects and reasons given while enacting the Act.
This Act was enacted with a purpose to regulate the purchase and
sale of agricultural produce, livestock and products of livestock and

the establishment of markets in connection therewith. In the
statement of objects and reasons, it is pointed out,
“The salient features of this Bill, among others, are;
i. to bring within the scope of the legislation all agricultural
commodities, both processed and unprocessed,
livestock and products of livestock.
ii. to empower the market committee to issue licences to
traders in a notified area.”
Therefore in the preamble, the legislature was clear that the purpose
of the bill was to bring within the scope of the legislation all
agricultural commodities both processed and unprocessed.
In a judgment reported in Biswambhar Singh & others V. State of
Orissa & another, a Constitutional Bench of Supreme Court held in
para 32;
“We are therefore bound to construe the ambiguous words which
we have examined above in a sense which will carry out the
purpose of the Act and not in a way which will travel beyond it.”
A Seven-Judge Bench of the Supreme Court in a Presidential
reference in In re The Kerala Education Bill, 1957, referred to the
judgment reported in Biswambhar Singh case (7 supra) and laid
down in para 19;
“the policy and purpose of a given measure may be deduced from
the long title and the preamble thereof has been recognised in
many decisions of this Court.”
In a judgment reported in N.K.Jain and others V. C.K.Shah and
others, the Supreme Court, while enunciating the principles of
interpretation, held;
“Legislative purpose must be noted and statute should be read as
a whole.”
In a judgment reported in State of Uttar Pradesh V. Tobit and others,

in para 3, the Supreme Court held;
“……….. it is well settled that “the words of a Statute, when there
is doubt about their meaning, are to be understood in the sense in
which they best harmonise with the subject of the enactment and
the object which the Legislature has in view. Their meaning is
found not so much in a strictly grammatical or etymological
propriety of language, nor even in its popular use, as in the subject
or in the occasion on which they are used, and the object to be
attained.” (Maxwell’s Interpretation of Statutes, 10
th Edition page
52). In order, therefore, to come to a decision as to the true
meaning of a word used in a Statute one has to enquire as to the
subject-matter of the enactment and the object which the
Legislature had in view.”
In a judgment reported in State of Madhya Pradesh V. M/s.Azad
Bharat Finance Co. and another, it is held;
“It is well recognized that if a statute leads to absurdity, hardship
or injustice, presumably not intended, a construction may be put
upon it which modifies the meaning of the words, and even the
structure of the sentence.”
The Supreme Court, in this connection, relied on a judgment reported
in Tirath Singh V. Bachittar Singh (1955-2 SCR 457/AIR 1955 SC
830).
In a judgment reported in Molar Mal (dead) through LRs., V. M/s.Kay
Iron Works (P) Ltd., the Supreme Court held;
“We agree with this contention of the landlord that normally the
Courts will have to follow the rule of literal construction which rule
enjoins the Court to take the words as used by the Legislature and
to give it the meaning which naturally implies. But, there is an
exception to this rule. That exception comes into play when
application of literal construction of the words in the Statute leads
to absurdity, inconsistency or when it is shown that the legal
context in which the words are used or by reading the Statute as a
whole, it requires a different meaning. In our opinion, if the
expression "entitled to apply again" is given its literal meaning, it
would defeat the very object for which the Legislature has

incorporated that proviso in the Act inasmuch as the object of that
proviso can be defeated by a landlord who has more than one
tenanted premises by filing multiple applications simultaneously
for eviction and thereafter obtain possession of all those premises
without the bar of the proviso being applicable to him. We are of
the opinion that this could not have been the purpose for which the
proviso is included in the Act. If such an interpretation is given
then the various provisos found in Clause (3) of Section 13 would
become otiose and the very object of the enactment would be
defeated. Any such interpretation, in our opinion, would lead to
absurdity. Therefore, we have no hesitation in interpreting the
proviso to mean that the restriction contemplated under that
proviso extends even up to the stage when the Court or the
tribunal is considering the case of the landlord for actual eviction
and is not confined to the stage of filing of eviction petition only.
In view of these judgments and in view of the other reasons
given hereinabove, we have no doubt in our mind that both the
processed and unprocessed agricultural produce can be agricultural
produce within the meaning of Section 2 (b) of the Act provided it is
notified in terms of the Act for the purpose of the Act by the
Government.
The learned Counsel for some of the petitioners
Mr.D.Sudhakar Rao has drawn our attention to a judgment reported
i n Srinivasa General Traders and others etc., V. State of Andhra
Pradesh and others, where the Supreme Court was dealing with the
question whether fee could be levied on rice and paddy. Though the
argument was not made which has been made before us, but at the
same time, we consider it to be a relevant judgment because, it also
considered the impact of Section 12(1) of the Act and in para 41 it
held;
“ 41. The view that no market fee is payable on purchase or sale
of rice stems from the premise that since paddy is de-husked into
rice there cannot be levy of market fee at both the stages i.e. on
purchase of paddy by a rice miller from a producer and again on
purchase or sale of rice by a rice miller to a trader or trader to a
trader. The question whether the fee is payable at both the stages
? It would all depend upon the scheme of each Act. The decision in
Ram Chandra’s case; (supra), turned on a construction of sub-

clause (2) of Section 17(iii)(b) of the Uttar Pradesh Krishi Utpadan
Mandi Adhiniyam, 1964, as amended by U.P.Act 7 of 1978. It was
conceded in that case on behalf of the State Government and the
market committees that there cannot be any multi-point levy of
market fee in the same market area. Under sub-cl. (2) of S.17 (iii)
(b) of that Act if an agricultural produce is purchased from a
producer directly, the trader is liable to pay market fee but when
the trader sells the same produce to another trader, neither the
seller nor the purchaser can be made to pay the market fee under
sub-clause (3). The scheme of the Act with which we are
concerned appears to be entirely different. Under sub-s. (1) of
Section 12 of the Act, a market committee is empowered to levy
market fee on any notified agricultural produce, livestock or
products of livestock purchased or sold in the notified market
area. It would appear that every purchase or sale of any notified
agricultural produce, livestock or products of livestock attracts the
levy of market fee. One is apt to think that rice and paddy are the
same commodity and therefore there is double taxation but, in
reality, it is not so. There is distinction between ‘paddy’ and ‘rice’
and although paddy is milled into rice by the process of de-husking, they are two separate and distinct commercial
commodities and have both been separately specified as notified
agricultural produce in Schedule II as items 1 and 2 respectively.
On the plain language of sub-s. (1) of S. 12 of the Act, the market
fee is leviable on both on purchase of paddy by a rice miller from a
producer and also on purchase or sale of rice by a miller to a
trader or by a trader to a trader because there is service rendered
by a market committee at each of the stages.”
For the reasons given hereinabove, we do not find merit in these Writ
Petitions, which are accordingly dismissed. No order as to costs.

(BILAL NAZKI, J)
21
st December, 2004.
